) with comparisons to the well-known value iteration (VI) method without the pessimism principle. To begin with, Fig.
2
(a) shows the average and standard derivations of the performance gap
V_{1}^{\star}(s)-V_{1}^{\widehat{\pi}}(s)
over all states
s\in\mathcal{S}
, over
10
independent experiments with a fixed sample size
N=50
. The results indicate that the proposed VI-LCB method outperforms the baseline VI method uniformly over the entire state space, showing that pessimism brings significant advantages in this sample-scarce regime.
Secondly, we evaluate the performance gap
V_{1}^{\star}(\rho)-V_{1}^{\widehat{\pi}}(\rho)
with varying sample size
N\in\big\{54,90,148,\cdots,22026\}\approx\{e^{4},e^{4.5},e^{5},\cdots,e^{10}\}
, over
10
independent trials. Note that throughout the experiments, we fix the parameter
c_{\mathsf{b}}=0.05
, which determines the level of the pessimism penalty of VI-LCB (cf. (
55
)). Fig.
2
(b) shows the average and standard derivations of the performance gap

V_{1}^{\star}(\rho)-V_{1}^{\widehat{\pi}}(\rho)
with respect to the sample size
N
. Clearly,
as the sample size increases, both our method VI-LCB and the baseline VI method perform better. Moreover, our VI-LCB method consistently outperforms the baseline VI method over the entire range of the sample size
N
, especially in the sample-starved regime. In addition, to corroborate the scaling of the sample size on the performance gap, we plot the sub-optimality performance gap of VI-LCB w.r.t. the sample size on a log-log scale in Fig.
2
(c). Fitting using linear regression leads to a slope estimate of
-0.502
, with the corresponding fitted line plotted in Fig.
2
(c) as well. This nicely matches the finding of Theorem
3
, which says the performance gap of VI-LCB scales as
N^{-1/2}
.

5
Related works

In this section, we provide further discussions about prior art, with an emphasis on settings that are most relevant to the current paper.

Off-policy evaluation and offline RL.

Broadly speaking, at least two families of problems have been investigated in the literature that tackle offline batch data: off-policy evaluation, where the goal is to estimate the value function of a target policy that deviates from the behavior policy used in data collection; and offline policy learning, where the goal is to identify a near-optimal policy (or at least an improved one compared to the behavior policy). Our work falls under the second category. A topic of its own interest, off-policy evaluation has been extensively studied in the recent literature; we excuse ourselves from enumerating the works in that space but only provide pointers to a few examples including
Uehara et al., 2020
;
Li et al., 2014
;
Yang et al., 2020
;
Duan et al., 2020
;
Jiang and Li, 2016
;
Jiang and Huang, 2020
;
Kallus and Uehara, 2020
;
Duan et al., 2021
;
Xu et al., 2021
;
Ren et al., 2021
;
Thomas and Brunskill, 2016
.

Offline RL with the pessimism principle.

The prior works that are the most relevant to this paper are
Rashidinejad et al., 2022
;
Xie et al., 2021
;
Jin et al., 2021
;
Shi et al., 2022b
;
Yan et al., 2023
;
Yin and Wang, 2021
,
which incorporated lower confidence bounds into value estimation in order to avoid overly uncertain regions not covered by the target policy.
In addition to the ones discussed in Section
1.2
that focus on minimax performance,
the recent works
Yin and Wang, 2021
;
Yin et al., 2022
further developed instance-dependent statistical guarantees for the pessimistic model-based approach.
The results in
Yin and Wang, 2021
, however, required a large burn-in sample size
\frac{H^{4}}{SC^{\star}(d^{\mathsf{b}}_{\min})^{2}}
(since
d^{\mathsf{b}}_{\min}
could be exceedingly small),
thus preventing it from attaining minimax optimality for the entire
\varepsilon
-range.
It is noteworthy that the principle of pessimism has been incorporated into policy optimization and actor-critic methods as well by searching for some least-favorable models (e.g.,
Uehara and Sun, 2021
;
Zanette et al., 2021
), which is quite different from the approach studied herein. On the empirical side, model-based algorithms
(
Yu et al., 2020
;
Kidambi et al., 2020
)
have been shown to achieve superior performance than their model-free counterpart for offline RL.
In addition, a number of recent works studied offline RL under various function approximation assumptions, e.g.,
Jin et al., 2021
;
Yin et al., 2022
;
Nguyen-Tang et al., 2021
;
Uehara and Sun, 2021
;
Uehara et al., 2022
;
Zhan et al., 2022
;
Zanette et al., 2021
, which are beyond the scope of the current paper.
Recently, the insights gleaned from the studies of offline RL have inspired improved algorithm designs for online and hybrid RL as well
(
Li et al., 2023
;
Li et al., 2024c
)
.

Online RL and the optimism principle.

The optimism principle in the face of uncertainty has received widespread adoption from bandits to online RL
(
Lai and Robbins, 1985
;
Lattimore and Szepesvári, 2020
;
Agarwal et al., 2021
)
.
In the context of online RL,

Jaksch et al., 2010

constructed confidence regions for the probability transition kernel to help select optimistic policies in the setting of weakly communicating MDPs,
based on a variant (called UCRL2) of the UCRL algorithm originally proposed in

Auer and Ortner, 2006
;
see also
Filippi et al., 2010
;
Bourel et al., 2020
;
Talebi and Maillard, 2018

for other variants of UCRL.
When applied to episodic finite-horizon MDPs, the regret bound in
Jaksch et al., 2010
was suboptimal by a factor of at least
\sqrt{H^{2}S}
; see discussion in
Azar et al., 2017
;
Jin et al., 2018
.

Fruit et al., 2020
developed an improved regret bound for UCRL2 by using empirical Bernstein-style bounds, which however was still suboptimal by a factor of at least
\sqrt{HS}
when specialized to episodic finite-horizon MDPs.
In comparison, a more sample-efficient paradigm is to build Bernstein-style UCBs for the optimal values to help select exploration policies,
which has been recently adopted in both model-based
(
Azar et al., 2017
;
Zhang et al., 2023
)
and model-free algorithms
(
Jin et al., 2018
)
.
Note that Bernstein-style uncertainty estimation alone is not enough to ensure regret optimality in model-free algorithms,
thereby motivating the design of more sophisticated variance reduction strategies
(
Li et al., 2021
;
Zhang et al., 2020
)
.
Finally, the optimism principle has been studied in undiscounted infinite-horizon MDPs too (e.g.,
Qian et al., 2019
),
which is beyond the scope of this paper.

Model-based RL.

The algorithms studied herein fall under the category of model-based RL, which decouples the model estimation and the planning.
This popular paradigm has been deployed and studied under various data collection mechanisms beyond offline RL,
including but not limited to the generative model (or simulator) setting
(
Azar et al., 2013
;
Agarwal et al., 2020
;
Li et al., 2024b
;
Li et al., 2020
)
and the online exploratory setting
(
Azar et al., 2017
;
Jin et al., 2020
;
Zhang et al., 2021a
;
Zhang et al., 2023
)
.
The leave-one-out analysis (and the construction of absorbing MDPs) adopted in the proof of Theorem
1

has been inspired by several recent works
Agarwal et al., 2020
;
Li et al., 2024b
;
Pananjady and Wainwright, 2020
;
Cui and Yang, 2021
,
and has recently been shown to be effective for multi-agent offline RL as well
(
Yan et al., 2022
)
.

Model-free RL.

Another widely used paradigm is model-free RL,
which attempts to learn the optimal value function without explicit construction of the model.
Arguably the most famous example of model-free RL is Q-learning,
which applies the stochastic approximation paradigm to find the fixed point of the Bellman operator
(
Watkins and Dayan, 1992
;
Even-Dar and Mansour, 2003
;
Beck and Srikant, 2012
;
Szepesvári, 1998
;
Murphy, 2005
;
Qu and Wierman, 2020
;
Li et al., 2024a
;
Li et al., 2022
;
Xiong et al., 2020
;
Shi et al., 2022a
;
Chen et al., 2020
)
.
It is worth noting that the asynchronous Q-learning, which aims to learn the optimal Q-function from a data trajectory collected by following a certain behavior policy,
shares some similarity with offline RL; note that prior results on vanilla asynchronous Q-learning require a strong uniform coverage requirement
(
Qu and Wierman, 2020
;
Li et al., 2024a
;
Chen et al., 2021b
)
, which is stronger than the single-policy concentrability considered herein.
Moreover, Q-learning alone is known to be sub-optimal in terms of the sample complexity in various settings
(
Li et al., 2024a
;
Jin et al., 2018
;
Shi et al., 2022b
;
Wainwright, 2019b
;
Bai et al., 2019
)
. This motivates the incorporation of the variance reduction in order to further improve the sample complexity
(
Wainwright, 2019c
;
Shi et al., 2022b
;
Yan et al., 2023
;
Li et al., 2021
;
Du et al., 2017
;
Zhang et al., 2021b
;
Li et al., 2022
;
Zhang et al., 2020
)
. Note, however, variance-reduced model-free RL typically requires a large burn-in cost in order to operate in a sample-optimal fashion, and is hence outperformed by the model-based approach under multiple sampling mechanisms.

6
Analysis: discounted infinite-horizon MDPs

This section is devoted to establishing Theorem
1
.
Towards this end, we claim that it is sufficient to prove the following theorem.

Theorem 5
.

Consider any
0<\delta<1
and any
\gamma\in[\frac{1}{2},1)
. Suppose that the penalty terms are set to be (
28
) for any numerical constant
c_{\mathsf{b}}\geq 144
.
Then with probability exceeding
1-2\delta,
for any estimate
\widehat{Q}
obeying
\big\|\widehat{Q}-\widehat{Q}^{\star}_{\mathsf{pe}}\big\|_{\infty}\leq 1/N
one has

\displaystyle V^{\star}(\rho)-V^{\widehat{\pi}}(\rho)\leq 120\sqrt{\frac{c_{\mathsf{b}}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NS}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)^{3}N}}+\frac{3464c_{\mathsf{b}}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NS}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)^{2}N},

(66)

where
\widehat{\pi}(s)\in\arg\max_{a}\widehat{Q}(s,a)
for any
s\in\mathcal{S}
.

As we have demonstrated in Lemma
2
, the output of Algorithm
1

satisfies
\big\|\widehat{Q}-\widehat{Q}^{\star}_{\mathsf{pe}}\big\|_{\infty}\leq 1/N
once the iteration number exceeds

\tau_{\max}\geq\frac{\log\frac{N}{1-\gamma}}{\log(1/\gamma)}
, thus making Theorem
5
applicable.
Taking the right-hand side of (
66
) to be no larger than
\varepsilon
reveals that:

V^{\star}(\rho)-V^{\widehat{\pi}}(\rho)\leq\varepsilon

holds as long as
N
exceeds

\displaystyle N\geq\frac{21000c_{\mathsf{b}}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NS}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)^{3}\varepsilon^{2}},

(67)

given that
\varepsilon\in\big(0,\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\big]
.

The remainder of this section is thus dedicated to establishing Theorem
5
.
Throughout the proof, it suffices to focus on the case where

\displaystyle N\geq\frac{c_{3}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NS}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{1-\gamma}

(68)

for some large constant
c_{3}\geq 2880000
;
otherwise the claim (
66
) follows directly since
V^{\star}(\rho)-V^{\widehat{\pi}}(\rho)\leq\frac{1}{1-\gamma}
.

6.1
Preliminary facts

Before embarking on the proof, we collect a couple of preliminary facts that will be used multiple times.

Properties of
N(s,a)
.

To begin with, the quantity
N(s,a)
— the total number of sample transitions from
(s,a)
— can be bounded through the following lemma; the proof is provided in Appendix
A.3
.

Lemma 4
.

Consider any
\delta\in(0,1)
. With probability at least
1-\delta
, the quantities
\{N(s,a)\}
in (
24
) obey

\displaystyle\max\Big\{N(s,a),\frac{2}{3}\log\frac{N}{\delta}\Big\}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{Nd^{\mathsf{b}}(s,a)}{12}

(69)

simultaneously for all
(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
.

Properties about
\widehat{V}
and
\widehat{V}^{\star}_{\mathsf{pe}}
.

First of all, note that the assumption

\displaystyle\big\|\widehat{Q}-\widehat{Q}^{\star}_{\mathsf{pe}}\big\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{N}

(70)

has the following direct consequence:

\displaystyle\big\|\widehat{V}-\widehat{V}^{\star}_{\mathsf{pe}}\big\|_{\infty}=\max_{s}\left|\max_{a}\widehat{Q}(s,a)-\max_{a}\widehat{Q}^{\star}_{\mathsf{pe}}(s,a)\right|\leq\big\|\widehat{Q}-\widehat{Q}^{\star}_{\mathsf{pe}}\big\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{N}.

(71)

Given the proximity of
\widehat{V}
and
\widehat{V}^{\star}_{\mathsf{pe}}
,
we can bound the difference of the corresponding variance terms as follows:

\displaystyle\left|\mathsf{Var}_{\widehat{P}_{s,a}}\big(\widehat{V}_{\mathsf{pe}}^{\star}\big)-\mathsf{Var}_{\widehat{P}_{s,a}}\big(\widehat{V}\big)\right|

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(i)}}{=}\left|\widehat{P}_{s,a}\big(\widehat{V}_{\mathsf{pe}}^{\star}\circ\widehat{V}_{\mathsf{pe}}^{\star}\big)-\widehat{P}_{s,a}\big(\widehat{V}\circ\widehat{V}\big)+\big(\widehat{P}_{s,a}\widehat{V}\big)^{2}-\big(\widehat{P}_{s,a}\widehat{V}_{\mathsf{pe}}^{\star}\big)^{2}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\widehat{P}_{s,a}\Big(\big(\widehat{V}+\widehat{V}_{\mathsf{pe}}^{\star}\big)\circ\big(\widehat{V}_{\mathsf{pe}}^{\star}-\widehat{V}\big)\Big)+\left(\widehat{P}_{s,a}\big(\widehat{V}+\widehat{V}_{\mathsf{pe}}^{\star}\big)\right)\left(\widehat{P}_{s,a}\big(\widehat{V}_{\mathsf{pe}}^{\star}-\widehat{V}\big)\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\big\|\widehat{P}_{s,a}\big\|_{1}\big\|\widehat{V}+\widehat{V}_{\mathsf{pe}}^{\star}\big\|_{\infty}\big\|\widehat{V}_{\mathsf{pe}}^{\star}-\widehat{V}\big\|_{\infty}+\big\|\widehat{P}_{s,a}\big\|_{1}^{2}\big\|\widehat{V}+\widehat{V}_{\mathsf{pe}}^{\star}\big\|_{\infty}\big\|\widehat{V}_{\mathsf{pe}}^{\star}-\widehat{V}\big\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\big\|\widehat{P}_{s,a}\big\|_{1}+\big\|\widehat{P}_{s,a}\big\|_{1}^{2}\right)\left(2\big\|\widehat{V}\big\|_{\infty}+\big\|\widehat{V}_{\mathsf{pe}}^{\star}-\widehat{V}\big\|_{\infty}\right)\big\|\widehat{V}_{\mathsf{pe}}^{\star}-\widehat{V}\big\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{N}\left(\frac{2}{1-\gamma}+\frac{1}{N}\right)\leq\frac{6}{(1-\gamma)N}.

(72)

Here, (i) follows from the definition (
8
), the penultimate inequality follows from (
71
) and the basic facts
\|\widehat{P}_{s,a}\|_{1}=1
and
\|\widehat{V}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{1-\gamma}
,
while the last line relies on (
68
).

Armed with (
72
), one can further control the difference of the associated penalty terms. Note that the definition of
b(s,a;V)
in (
28
) tells us that

\displaystyle\left|b\big(s,a;\widehat{V}_{\mathsf{pe}}^{\star}\big)-b\big(s,a;\widehat{V}\big)\right|

\displaystyle=\Bigg|\min\Bigg\{\max\bigg\{\sqrt{\frac{c_{\mathsf{b}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{N(s,a)}\mathsf{Var}_{\widehat{P}_{s,a}}\big(\widehat{V}_{\mathsf{pe}}^{\star}\big)},\frac{2c_{\mathsf{b}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)N(s,a)}\bigg\},\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\qquad-\min\Bigg\{\max\bigg\{\sqrt{\frac{c_{\mathsf{b}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{N(s,a)}\mathsf{Var}_{\widehat{P}_{s,a}}\big(\widehat{V}\big)},\frac{2c_{\mathsf{b}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)N(s,a)}\bigg\},\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\Bigg\}\Bigg|.

(73)

If at least one of the variance terms is not too small in the sense that

\max\left\{\mathsf{Var}_{\widehat{P}_{s,a}}\big(\widehat{V}_{\mathsf{pe}}^{\star}\big),\mathsf{Var}_{\widehat{P}_{s,a}}\big(\widehat{V}\big)\right\}\geq\frac{4c_{\mathsf{b}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)^{2}N(s,a)},

(74)

then (
73
) implies that

(
73
)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{c_{\mathsf{b}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{N(s,a)}}\left|\sqrt{\mathsf{Var}_{\widehat{P}_{s,a}}\big(\widehat{V}_{\mathsf{pe}}^{\star}\big)}-\sqrt{\mathsf{Var}_{\widehat{P}_{s,a}}\big(\widehat{V}\big)}\right|=\sqrt{\frac{c_{\mathsf{b}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{N(s,a)}}\frac{\big|\mathsf{Var}_{\widehat{P}_{s,a}}\big(\widehat{V}_{\mathsf{pe}}^{\star}\big)-\mathsf{Var}_{\widehat{P}_{s,a}}\big(\widehat{V}\big)\big|}{\sqrt{\mathsf{Var}_{\widehat{P}_{s,a}}\big(\widehat{V}_{\mathsf{pe}}^{\star}\big)}+\sqrt{\mathsf{Var}_{\widehat{P}_{s,a}}\big(\widehat{V}\big)}}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(i)}}{\leq}\frac{1-\gamma}{2}\Big|\mathsf{Var}_{\widehat{P}_{s,a}}\big(\widehat{V}_{\mathsf{pe}}^{\star}\big)-\mathsf{Var}_{\widehat{P}_{s,a}}\big(\widehat{V}\big)\Big|\overset{\mathrm{(ii)}}{\leq}\frac{3}{N},

(75)

where (i) results from (
74
), and (ii) holds due to (
72
).
On the other hand, if (
74
) is not satisfied, then one clearly has

b\big(s,a;\widehat{V}_{\mathsf{pe}}^{\star}\big)=b\big(s,a;\widehat{V}\big)
. In conclusion, in all cases we have

\displaystyle\left|b\big(s,a;\widehat{V}_{\mathsf{pe}}^{\star}\big)-b\big(s,a;\widehat{V}\big)\right|\leq\frac{3}{N}.

(76)

6.2
Proof of Theorem
5

Armed with the preceding preliminary facts, we can readily turn to the proof of Theorem
5
.
By virtue of Lemma
4
, our proof shall — unless otherwise noted — operate on the high-probability event that

\displaystyle\forall(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}:\qquad\max\bigg\{N(s,a),\frac{2}{3}\log\frac{SN}{\delta}\bigg\}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{Nd^{\mathsf{b}}(s,a)}{12}.

(77)

In addition, from the sampling model (
17
),
the sample transitions employed to form
\widehat{P}
are statistically independent conditional on
\{N(s,a)\}
.
Our proof consists of four steps as detailed below.

Step 1: Bernstein-style inequalities and leave-one-out decoupling argument.

We are in need of tight control of the size of

\big(\widehat{P}_{s,a}-P_{s,a}\big)\widehat{V}
.
However, this becomes challenging due to the statistical dependency between
\widehat{P}
and the value estimate
\widehat{V}
(given that we reuse samples in all iterations of Algorithm
1
).
In order to circumvent this difficulty, we resort to a leave-one-out argument to decouple the statistical dependency,
as motivated by
Agarwal et al., 2020
;
Li et al., 2024b
. The result stated below establishes Bernstein-style inequalities despite the complicated dependency.

Lemma 5
.

Suppose that
\gamma\in[\frac{1}{2},1)
, and consider any
\delta\in(0,1)
.
With probability at least
1-\delta
, we have

\displaystyle\Big|\big(\widehat{P}_{s,a}-P_{s,a}\big)\widetilde{V}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq 12\sqrt{\frac{\log\frac{2N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{N(s,a)}\mathsf{Var}_{\widehat{P}_{s,a}}\big(\widetilde{V}\big)}+\frac{74\log\frac{2N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)N(s,a)},

(78a)

\displaystyle\mathsf{Var}_{\widehat{P}_{s,a}}\big(\widetilde{V}\big)

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathsf{Var}_{P_{s,a}}\big(\widetilde{V}\big)+\frac{41\log\frac{2N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)^{2}N(s,a)}

(78b)

simultaneously for all
(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}

and all
\widetilde{V}
with
\big\|\widetilde{V}-\widehat{V}^{\star}_{\mathsf{pe}}\big\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{N}
and
\|\widetilde{V}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{1-\gamma}
.

High-level proof ideas.

In short, the proof consists of contructing a finite collection of auxiliary MDPs
\{\widehat{\mathcal{M}}^{s,u}\}
for each state
s
obeying the following properties:
(i) each
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}^{s,u}
is constructed without using any sample transition that comes from state
s
, and is hence statistically independent from
\widehat{P}_{s,a}
for all
a\in\mathcal{A}

(instead, the useful information is embedded into the corresponding immediate reward, which is a low-dimensional object and easier to control);
(ii) at least one of the MDPs in
\{\widehat{\mathcal{M}}^{s,u}\}
is extremely close to the true MDP in terms of the resulting value function.
With the aid of these leave-one-out auxiliary MDPs, one can control
\big(\widehat{P}_{s,a}-P_{s,a}\big)\widetilde{V}

by first exploiting the statistical independence between
\widehat{P}_{s,a}
and
\{\widehat{\mathcal{M}}^{s,u}\}
and then transferring the concentration bound back to the original MDP using the proximity property (ii).
The construction of these auxiliary MDPs and the proof details can be found in Appendix
A.4
.
∎

Note that (
78a
) has been derived only for those pairs
(s,a)
with
N(s,a)>0
.
For every
(s,a)
with
N(s,a)=0
, one can directly obtain

\Big|\big(\widehat{P}_{s,a}-P_{s,a}\big)\widetilde{V}\Big|=\big|P_{s,a}\widetilde{V}\big|\leq\big\|P_{s,a}\big\|_{1}\big\|\widetilde{V}\big\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{1-\gamma}.

Putting these bounds together with the definition (
28
) of
b(s,a;V)
reveals that

\displaystyle\Big|\big(\widehat{P}_{s,a}-P_{s,a}\big)\widetilde{V}\Big|+\frac{5}{N}\leq b\big(s,a;\widetilde{V}\big)\qquad\text{for all }(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}

(79)

for all
\widetilde{V}
obeying
\big\|\widetilde{V}-\widehat{V}^{\star}_{\mathsf{pe}}\big\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{N}
and
\|\widetilde{V}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{1-\gamma}
,
provided that the constant
c_{\mathsf{b}}
is sufficiently large.
The remainder of the proof should then also operate on the high-probability events (
79
) and (
78b
), in addition to assuming that the event (
77
) occurs.

Step 2: showing that
\widehat{Q}(s,a)
is a lower bound on
Q^{\widehat{\pi}}(s,a)
.

We now justify that
\widehat{Q}(s,a)
(resp.
\widehat{V}(s)
) is a “pessimistic” estimate of
Q^{\widehat{\pi}}(s,a)
(resp.
V^{\widehat{\pi}}(s)
);
this is enabled by the pessimism principle (so that the algorithm effectively seeks lower estimates of the value iteration) and the Bernstein-style bounds in Lemma
5
(so that the penalty term always dominates the uncertainty incurred by using the empirical MDP).

To begin with, recall that
\widehat{Q}^{\star}_{\mathsf{pe}}(s,a)
is the unique fixed point of the pessimistic Bellman operator that obeys

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}^{\star}_{\mathsf{pe}}(s,a)=\max\Big\{r(s,a)+\gamma\widehat{P}_{s,a}\widehat{V}^{\star}_{\mathsf{pe}}-b\big(s,a;\widehat{V}^{\star}_{\mathsf{pe}}\big),0\Big\}.

(80)

In the sequel, we divide the set of state-action pairs
(s,a)
into two types.

•

Case 1:
\widehat{Q}^{\star}_{\mathsf{pe}}(s,a)=0
.
Given that
\widehat{Q}_{0}=0
, Lemma
2
tells us that

\widehat{Q}(s,a)=\widehat{Q}_{\tau_{\max}}(s,a)\leq\widehat{Q}^{\star}_{\mathsf{pe}}(s,a)=0.

This combined with the basic fact
Q^{\widehat{\pi}}\geq 0
immediately yields
0=\widehat{Q}(s,a)\leq Q^{\widehat{\pi}}(s,a)
.

•

Case 2:
\widehat{Q}^{\star}_{\mathsf{pe}}(s,a)=r(s,a)+\gamma\widehat{P}_{s,a}\widehat{V}^{\star}_{\mathsf{pe}}-b\big(s,a;\widehat{V}^{\star}_{\mathsf{pe}}\big)>0
.
It is first observed that

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}(s,a)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(i)}}{\leq}\widehat{Q}^{\star}_{\mathsf{pe}}(s,a)+\frac{1}{N}\overset{\text{(ii)}}{=}r(s,a)-b\big(s,a;\widehat{V}^{\star}_{\mathsf{pe}}\big)+\gamma\widehat{P}_{s,a}\widehat{V}^{\star}_{\mathsf{pe}}+\frac{1}{N}

\displaystyle\leq r(s,a)-b\big(s,a;\widehat{V}^{\star}_{\mathsf{pe}}\big)+\gamma\widehat{P}_{s,a}\widehat{V}+\frac{1}{N}+\gamma\big\|\widehat{P}_{s,a}\big\|_{1}\big\|\widehat{V}-\widehat{V}_{\mathsf{pe}}^{\star}\big\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(iii)}}{\leq}r(s,a)-b\big(s,a;\widehat{V}^{\star}_{\mathsf{pe}}\big)+\gamma\widehat{P}_{s,a}\widehat{V}+\frac{2}{N}

\displaystyle\leq r(s,a)-b\big(s,a;\widehat{V}\big)+\gamma P_{s,a}\widehat{V}+\frac{2}{N}+\gamma\Big|\big(\widehat{P}_{s,a}-P_{s,a}\big)\widehat{V}\Big|+\left|b\big(s,a;\widehat{V}_{\mathsf{pe}}^{\star}\big)-b\big(s,a;\widehat{V}\big)\right|

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(iv)}}{\leq}r(s,a)+\gamma P_{s,a}\widehat{V}.

(81)

Here, (i) and (iii) arise from the assumption (
70
), (ii) relies on the fact that
\widehat{Q}_{\mathsf{pe}}^{\star}
is the fixed point of the operator
\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pe}}
,
whereas (iv) takes advantage of (
76
) and (
79
).
Combining (
81
) with the Bellman equation
Q^{\widehat{\pi}}=r+\gamma PV^{\widehat{\pi}}
results in

\displaystyle Q^{\widehat{\pi}}(s,a)-\widehat{Q}(s,a)\geq r(s,a)+\gamma P_{s,a}V^{\widehat{\pi}}-\big(r(s,a)+\gamma P_{s,a}\widehat{V}\big)=\gamma P_{s,a}\big(V^{\widehat{\pi}}-\widehat{V}\big).

(82)

Suppose for the moment that there exists some
(s,a)
obeying
Q^{\widehat{\pi}}(s,a)-\widehat{Q}(s,a)<0
(which clearly cannot happen in Case 1),
then
\arg\min_{s,a}\big[Q^{\widehat{\pi}}(s,a)-\widehat{Q}(s,a)\big]
must belong to Case 2.
Thus, taking the minimum over
(s,a)
and using the above inequality (
82
) give

\displaystyle\min_{s,a}\big[Q^{\widehat{\pi}}(s,a)-\widehat{Q}(s,a)\big]

\displaystyle\geq\min_{s,a}\big[\gamma P_{s,a}\big(V^{\widehat{\pi}}-\widehat{V}\big)\big]\overset{\text{(i)}}{\geq}\gamma\min_{s}\big[V^{\widehat{\pi}}(s)-\widehat{V}(s)\big]

\displaystyle=\gamma\min_{s}\big[Q^{\widehat{\pi}}\big(s,\widehat{\pi}(s)\big)-\widehat{Q}\big(s,\widehat{\pi}(s)\big)\big]\geq\gamma\min_{s,a}\big[Q^{\widehat{\pi}}(s,a)-\widehat{Q}(s,a)\big],

(83)

where (i) holds since
P_{s,a}\in\Delta(\mathcal{S})
.
Given that
1>\gamma>0
, inequality (
83
) holds only when

\min_{s,a}\big[Q^{\widehat{\pi}}(s,a)-\widehat{Q}(s,a)\big]\geq 0
.
We therefore conclude that in this case, one also has
Q^{\widehat{\pi}}(s,a)\geq\widehat{Q}(s,a)
.

With the arguments for the above two cases in place, we arrive at

Q^{\widehat{\pi}}(s,a)\geq\widehat{Q}(s,a)\qquad\text{for all }(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A},

(84)

and evidently,

V^{\star}(s)\geq V^{\widehat{\pi}}(s)=Q^{\widehat{\pi}}\big(s,\widehat{\pi}(s)\big)\geq\widehat{Q}\big(s,\widehat{\pi}(s)\big)=\max_{a}\widehat{Q}(s,a)=\widehat{V}(s)\qquad\text{for all }s\in\mathcal{S}.

(85)

Step 3: bounding
V^{\star}(s)-V^{\widehat{\pi}}(s)
.

Recall that the Bellman optimality equation gives

\displaystyle V^{\star}(s)=r\big(s,\pi^{\star}(s)\big)+\gamma P_{s,\pi^{\star}(s)}V^{\star}.

(86)

Before continuing, we make note of the following lower bound on
\widehat{V}
:

\displaystyle\widehat{V}(s)

\displaystyle=\max_{a}\widehat{Q}(s,a)\geq\widehat{Q}\big(s,\pi^{\star}(s)\big)\overset{\mathrm{(i)}}{\geq}\widehat{Q}^{\star}_{\mathsf{pe}}\big(s,\pi^{\star}(s)\big)-\frac{1}{N}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(ii)}}{\geq}r\big(s,\pi^{\star}(s)\big)-b\big(s,\pi^{\star}(s);\widehat{V}^{\star}_{\mathsf{pe}}\big)+\gamma\widehat{P}_{s,\pi^{\star}(s)}\widehat{V}_{\mathsf{pe}}^{\star}-\frac{1}{N}

\displaystyle=r\big(s,\pi^{\star}(s)\big)-b\big(s,\pi^{\star}(s);\widehat{V}^{\star}_{\mathsf{pe}}\big)+\gamma\widehat{P}_{s,\pi^{\star}(s)}\widehat{V}-\frac{1}{N}-\gamma\widehat{P}_{s,\pi^{\star}(s)}\big(\widehat{V}-\widehat{V}_{\mathsf{pe}}^{\star}\big)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(iii)}}{\geq}r\big(s,\pi^{\star}(s)\big)-b\big(s,\pi^{\star}(s);\widehat{V}^{\star}_{\mathsf{pe}}\big)+\gamma\widehat{P}_{s,\pi^{\star}(s)}\widehat{V}-\frac{2}{N}

\displaystyle\geq r\big(s,\pi^{\star}(s)\big)-b\big(s,\pi^{\star}(s);\widehat{V}\big)+\gamma P_{s,\pi^{\star}(s)}\widehat{V}-\frac{2}{N}-\gamma\Big|\big(\widehat{P}_{s,\pi^{\star}(s)}-P_{s,\pi^{\star}(s)}\big)\widehat{V}\Big|

\displaystyle\qquad-\left|b\big(s,\pi^{\star}(s);\widehat{V}_{\mathsf{pe}}^{\star}\big)-b\big(s,\pi^{\star}(s);\widehat{V}\big)\right|

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(iv)}}{\geq}r\big(s,\pi^{\star}(s)\big)-2b\big(s,\pi^{\star}(s);\widehat{V}\big)+\gamma P_{s,\pi^{\star}(s)}\widehat{V}.

(87)

Here, (i) results from the assumption (
70
),
(ii) relies on (
80
),
(iii) is valid since
\widehat{P}_{s,\pi^{\star}(s)}\big(\widehat{V}-\widehat{V}_{\mathsf{pe}}^{\star}\big)\leq\big\|\widehat{P}_{s,\pi^{\star}(s)}\big\|_{1}\big\|\widehat{V}-\widehat{V}_{\mathsf{pe}}^{\star}\big\|_{\infty}\leq 1/N
,
whereas (iv) holds by virtue of (
76
) and (
79
).
Armed with the results in (
86
) and (
87
), we can readily show that

\displaystyle\big\langle\rho,V^{\star}-\widehat{V}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\rho(s)\big(V^{\star}(s)-\widehat{V}(s)\big)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\rho(s)\left\{r\big(s,\pi^{\star}(s)\big)+\gamma P_{s,\pi^{\star}(s)}V^{\star}-\left(r\big(s,\pi^{\star}(s)\big)-2b\big(s,\pi^{\star}(s);\widehat{V}\big)+\gamma P_{s,\pi^{\star}(s)}\widehat{V}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\rho(s)P_{s,\pi^{\star}(s)}\big(V^{\star}-\widehat{V}\big)+2\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\rho(s)b\big(s,\pi^{\star}(s);\widehat{V}\big).

(88)

For notational convenience, let us introduce a matrix
P^{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{S\times S}
and a vector
b^{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{S\times 1}

whose
s
-th row are given respectively by

\displaystyle\big[P^{\star}\big]_{s,\cdot}\coloneqq P_{s,\pi^{\star}(s)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad b^{\star}(s)\coloneqq b\big(s,\pi^{\star}(s);\widehat{V}\big)\qquad\quad\text{for all }s\in\mathcal{S}.

(89)

This allows us to rewrite (
88
) in the following matrix/vector form:

\displaystyle\rho^{\top}\big(V^{\star}-\widehat{V}\big)\leq\gamma\rho^{\top}P^{\star}\big(V^{\star}-\widehat{V}\big)+2\rho^{\top}b^{\star}.

(90)

Note that this relation holds for any arbitrary
\rho
. Apply it recursively to arrive at

\displaystyle\rho^{\top}\big(V^{\star}-\widehat{V}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\big(\gamma\rho^{\top}P^{\star}\big)\big(V^{\star}-\widehat{V}\big)+2\rho^{\top}b^{\star}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\big(\gamma\rho^{\top}P^{\star}\big)P^{\star}\big(V^{\star}-\widehat{V}\big)+2\big(\gamma\rho^{\top}P^{\star}\big)b^{\star}+2\rho^{\top}b^{\star}

\displaystyle=\gamma^{2}\rho^{\top}\big(P^{\star}\big)^{2}\big(V^{\star}-\widehat{V}\big)+2\gamma\rho^{\top}P^{\star}b^{\star}+2\rho^{\top}b^{\star}

\displaystyle\leq\cdots\leq\left\{\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}\gamma^{i}\rho^{\top}\big(P^{\star}\big)^{i}\big(V^{\star}-\widehat{V}\big)\right\}+2\rho^{\top}\bigg\{\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{i}\big(P^{\star}\big)^{i}\bigg\}b^{\star}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(i)}}{=}2\rho^{\top}\bigg\{\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{i}\big(P^{\star}\big)^{i}\bigg\}b^{\star}=2\rho^{\top}\big(I-\gamma P^{\star}\big)^{-1}b^{\star}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{1-\gamma}\langle d^{\star},b^{\star}\rangle,

(91)

where (i) holds since
\lim_{i\to\infty}\gamma^{i}\rho^{\top}\big(P^{\star}\big)^{i}\big(V^{\star}-\widehat{V}\big)=0
(given that
\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}\gamma^{i}=0
and
\|\rho^{\top}\big(P^{\star}\big)^{i}\|_{1}=1
for any
i\geq 0
), and the last equality results from the definition of
d^{\star}
(see (
16
)) expressed in the following matrix/vector form:

\displaystyle\big(d^{\star}\big)^{\top}=(1-\gamma)\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}\rho^{\top}\big(P^{\star}\big)^{t}=(1-\gamma)\rho^{\top}\big(I-\gamma P^{\star}\big)^{-1}.

(92)

Combine the above inequality with (
85
) to reach

\displaystyle\big\langle\rho,V^{\star}-V^{\widehat{\pi}}\big\rangle\leq\big\langle\rho,V^{\star}-\widehat{V}\big\rangle\leq\frac{2\big\langle d^{\star},b^{\star}\big\rangle}{1-\gamma}.

(93)

Step 4: using concentrability to control
\big\langle d^{\star},b^{\star}\big\rangle
.

We shall control
\big\langle d^{\star},b^{\star}\big\rangle
by dividing the state set
\mathcal{S}
into the following two disjoint subsets:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{small}}\coloneqq\bigg\{s\in\mathcal{S}\mid Nd^{\mathsf{b}}\big(s,\pi^{\star}(s)\big)\leq 8\log\frac{NS}{(1-\gamma)\delta}\bigg\};

(94a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{large}}\coloneqq\bigg\{s\in\mathcal{S}\mid Nd^{\mathsf{b}}\big(s,\pi^{\star}(s)\big)>8\log\frac{NS}{(1-\gamma)\delta}\bigg\}.

(94b)

•

To begin with, consider any state
s\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{small}}
. Applying Definition
2
and the definition of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{small}}
yields

\displaystyle\min\Big\{d^{\star}(s),\frac{1}{S}\Big\}\leq C^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}d^{\mathsf{b}}\big(s,\pi^{\star}(s)\big)\leq\frac{8C^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NS}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{N}<\frac{1}{S},

(95)

provided that
N>8SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NS}{(1-\gamma)\delta}
(see (
68
)). This inequality necessarily implies that

\displaystyle d^{\star}(s)\leq\frac{8C^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NS}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{N}<\frac{1}{S}.

(96)

Combining the preceding inequality with the following fact (see the definition (
28
))

\displaystyle b^{\star}(s)\coloneqq b\big(s,\pi^{\star}(s);\widehat{V}\big)\leq\frac{1}{1-\gamma}+\frac{5}{N},

(97)

we arrive at

\displaystyle\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{small}}}d^{\star}(s)b^{\star}(s)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{small}}}\left(\frac{8C^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NS}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)N}+d^{\star}(s)\frac{5}{N}\right)\leq\frac{8SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NS}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)N}+\frac{5}{N}.

(98)

•

Next, we turn to any state
s\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{large}}
. Using the definition (
28
) of
b(s,a;V)
, we obtain

\displaystyle b^{\star}(s)

\displaystyle=b\big(s,\pi^{\star}(s);\widehat{V}\big)\leq\sqrt{\frac{c_{\mathsf{b}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{N\big(s,\pi^{\star}(s)\big)}\mathsf{Var}_{\widehat{P}_{s,\pi^{\star}(s)}}\big(\widehat{V}\big)}+\frac{2c_{\mathsf{b}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)N\big(s,\pi^{\star}(s)\big)}+\frac{5}{N}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(i)}}{\leq}\sqrt{\frac{c_{\mathsf{b}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{N\big(s,\pi^{\star}(s)\big)}\left(2\mathsf{Var}_{P_{s,\pi^{\star}(s)}}\big(\widehat{V}\big)+\frac{41\log\frac{2N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)^{2}N\big(s,\pi^{\star}(s)\big)}\right)}+\frac{2c_{\mathsf{b}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)N\big(s,\pi^{\star}(s)\big)}+\frac{5}{N}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(ii)}}{\leq}\sqrt{\frac{2c_{\mathsf{b}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{N\big(s,\pi^{\star}(s)\big)}\mathsf{Var}_{P_{s,\pi^{\star}(s)}}\big(\widehat{V}\big)}+\frac{4c_{\mathsf{b}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)N\big(s,\pi^{\star}(s)\big)},

(99)

where (i) arises from Lemma
5
and (
71
), (ii) applies the elementary inequality
\sqrt{x+y}\leq\sqrt{x}+\sqrt{y}
for any
x,y\geq 0
and the fact
N\geq N(s,a)
, in addition to assuming that
c_{\mathsf{b}}
is large enough. To continue, we observe that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N\big(s,\pi^{\star}(s)\big)}\overset{\mathrm{(i)}}{\leq}\frac{12}{Nd^{\mathsf{b}}\big(s,\pi^{\star}(s)\big)}\overset{\mathrm{(ii)}}{\leq}\frac{12C^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}}{N\min\big\{d^{\star}(s),\frac{1}{S}\big\}}\leq\frac{12C^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}}{N}\left(\frac{1}{d^{\star}(s)}+S\right),

(100)

where (i) follows from the assumption (
77
) and the definition of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{large}}
,
and (ii) results from Assumption
2
.
Substitution into (
99
) yields

\displaystyle b^{\star}(s)

\displaystyle\leq\underset{\eqqcolon\,\alpha_{1}(s)}{\underbrace{\sqrt{\frac{24c_{\mathsf{b}}C^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{N}\mathsf{Var}_{P_{s,\pi^{\star}(s)}}\big(\widehat{V}\big)}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d^{\star}(s)}}+\sqrt{S}\right)}}+\underset{\eqqcolon\,\alpha_{2}(s)}{\underbrace{\frac{48c_{\mathsf{b}}C^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)N}\left(\frac{1}{d^{\star}(s)}+S\right)}},

(101)

where the last line comes from the elementary inequality
\sqrt{x+y}\leq\sqrt{x}+\sqrt{y}
for any
x,y\geq 0
.

To proceed, observe that the sum of the first terms in (
101
) satisfies

\displaystyle\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{large}}}d^{\star}(s)\alpha_{1}(s)

\displaystyle\quad=\sqrt{\frac{24c_{\mathsf{b}}C^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{N}}\left(\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{large}}}\sqrt{d^{\star}(s)\mathsf{Var}_{P_{s,\pi^{\star}(s)}}\big(\widehat{V}\big)}+\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{large}}}\sqrt{d^{\star}(s)}\sqrt{Sd^{\star}(s)\mathsf{Var}_{P_{s,\pi^{\star}(s)}}\big(\widehat{V}\big)}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\overset{\mathrm{(i)}}{\leq}\sqrt{\frac{24c_{\mathsf{b}}C^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{N}}\Bigg(\sqrt{S}\cdot\sqrt{\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{large}}}d^{\star}(s)\mathsf{Var}_{P_{s,\pi^{\star}(s)}}\big(\widehat{V}\big)}+\sqrt{\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{large}}}Sd^{\star}(s)\mathsf{Var}_{P_{s,\pi^{\star}(s)}}\big(\widehat{V}\big)}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\quad=\sqrt{\frac{96c_{\mathsf{b}}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{N}}\sqrt{\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{large}}}d^{\star}(s)\mathsf{Var}_{P_{s,\pi^{\star}(s)}}\big(\widehat{V}\big)},

(102)

where (i) arises from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the fact
\sum_{s}d^{\star}(s)=1
.
In addition, it is easily verified that the sum of the second terms in (
101
) obeys

\displaystyle\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{large}}}d^{\star}(s)\alpha_{2}(s)\leq\frac{96c_{\mathsf{b}}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)N},

(103)

which also makes use of the identity
\sum_{s}d^{\star}(s)=1
.
Combining (
102
) and (
103
) with (
101
) gives

\displaystyle\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{large}}}d^{\star}(s)b^{\star}\big(s,\pi^{\star}(s)\big)\leq\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{large}}}d^{\star}(s)\alpha_{1}(s)+\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{large}}}d^{\star}(s)\alpha_{2}(s)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\sqrt{\frac{96c_{\mathsf{b}}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{N}}\sqrt{\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{large}}}d^{\star}(s)\mathsf{Var}_{P_{s,\pi^{\star}(s)}}\big(\widehat{V}\big)}+\frac{96c_{\mathsf{b}}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)N}.

(104)

The above results (
98
) and (
104
) taken collectively give

\displaystyle\big\langle d^{\star},b^{\star}\big\rangle=\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{large}}}d^{\star}(s)b^{\star}(s)+\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{small}}}d^{\star}(s)b^{\star}(s)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\sqrt{\frac{96c_{\mathsf{b}}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{N}}\sqrt{\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{large}}}d^{\star}(s)\mathsf{Var}_{P_{s,\pi^{\star}(s)}}\big(\widehat{V}\big)}+\frac{96c_{\mathsf{b}}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)N}

\displaystyle\quad\qquad+\frac{8SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NS}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)N}+\frac{5}{N}

\displaystyle\quad\overset{\mathrm{(i)}}{\leq}\sqrt{\frac{96c_{\mathsf{b}}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NS}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{N}}\sqrt{\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}d^{\star}(s)\mathsf{Var}_{P_{s,\pi^{\star}(s)}}\big(\widehat{V}\big)}+\frac{98c_{\mathsf{b}}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NS}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)N}

\displaystyle\quad\overset{\mathrm{(ii)}}{\leq}\frac{2}{\gamma}\sqrt{\frac{96c_{\mathsf{b}}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NS}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)N}\big\langle d^{\star},b^{\star}\big\rangle}+\frac{1}{\gamma}\sqrt{\frac{192c_{\mathsf{b}}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NS}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)N}}+\frac{98c_{\mathsf{b}}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NS}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)N},

\displaystyle\quad\overset{\mathrm{(iii)}}{\leq}4\sqrt{\frac{96c_{\mathsf{b}}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NS}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)N}\big\langle d^{\star},b^{\star}\big\rangle}+2\sqrt{\frac{192c_{\mathsf{b}}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NS}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)N}}+\frac{98c_{\mathsf{b}}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NS}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)N},

\displaystyle\quad\overset{\mathrm{(iv)}}{\leq}\frac{1}{2}\big\langle d^{\star},b^{\star}\big\rangle+\frac{768c_{\mathsf{b}}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NS}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)N}+\sqrt{\frac{768c_{\mathsf{b}}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NS}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)N}}+\frac{98c_{\mathsf{b}}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NS}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)N}.

Here, (i) follows when
c_{\mathsf{b}}
is sufficiently large and
C^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\geq 1/S
(see (
21
)),
(ii) would hold as long as the following inequality could be established:

\displaystyle\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}d^{\star}(s)\mathsf{Var}_{P_{s,\pi^{\star}(s)}}\big(\widehat{V}\big)\leq\frac{2}{\gamma^{2}(1-\gamma)}+\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}(1-\gamma)}\big\langle d^{\star},b^{\star}\big\rangle;

(105)

(iii) is valid since
\gamma\in[\frac{1}{2},1)
, and (iv) follows from the elementary inequality
2xy\leq x^{2}+y^{2}
.
Rearranging terms, we are left with

\displaystyle\big\langle d^{\star},b^{\star}\big\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{3072c_{\mathsf{b}}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NS}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)N}}+\frac{1732c_{\mathsf{b}}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NS}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)N},

(106)

which combined with (
93
) yields

\displaystyle\big\langle\rho,V^{\star}-V^{\widehat{\pi}}\big\rangle\leq\frac{2\big\langle d^{\star},b^{\star}\big\rangle}{1-\gamma}\leq 120\sqrt{\frac{c_{\mathsf{b}}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NS}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)^{3}N}}+\frac{3464c_{\mathsf{b}}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NS}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)^{2}N}.

(107)

This concludes the proof, as long as the inequality (
105
) can be established.

Proof of inequality (
105
).

To begin with, we make the observation that

\displaystyle\big(\widehat{V}\circ\widehat{V}\big)-\big(\gamma P^{\star}\widehat{V})\circ\big(\gamma P^{\star}\widehat{V})

\displaystyle=\big(\widehat{V}-\gamma P^{\star}\widehat{V}\big)\circ\big(\widehat{V}+\gamma P^{\star}\widehat{V}\big)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(i)}}{\leq}\big(\widehat{V}-\gamma P^{\star}\widehat{V}+2b^{\star}\big)\circ\big(\widehat{V}+\gamma P^{\star}\widehat{V}\big)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(ii)}}{\leq}\frac{2}{1-\gamma}\big(\widehat{V}-\gamma P^{\star}\widehat{V}+2b^{\star}\big),

(108)

where (i) holds since
b^{\star}\geq 0
and
\widehat{V}+\gamma P^{\star}\widehat{V}\geq 0
, (ii) follows from the basic property
\|\widehat{V}+\gamma P^{\star}\widehat{V}\|_{\infty}\leq 2\|\widehat{V}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{2}{1-\gamma}
and the fact
\widehat{V}-\gamma P^{\star}\widehat{V}+2b^{\star}\geq 0
, the latter of which has been verified in (
87
).
Armed with this fact, one can deduce that

\displaystyle\sum_{s}d^{\star}(s)\mathsf{Var}_{P_{s,\pi^{\star}(s)}}\big(\widehat{V}\big)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(i)}}{=}\left\langle d^{\star},P^{\star}\big(\widehat{V}\circ\widehat{V}\big)-\big(P^{\star}\widehat{V}\big)\circ\big(P^{\star}\widehat{V}\big)\right\rangle

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(ii)}}{\leq}\left\langle d^{\star},P^{\star}\big(\widehat{V}\circ\widehat{V}\big)-\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}}\widehat{V}\circ\widehat{V}+\frac{2}{\gamma^{2}(1-\gamma)}\big(\widehat{V}-\gamma P^{\star}\widehat{V}+2b^{\star}\big)\right\rangle

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(iii)}}{\leq}\left\langle d^{\star},P^{\star}\big(\widehat{V}\circ\widehat{V}\big)-\frac{1}{\gamma}\widehat{V}\circ\widehat{V}+\frac{2}{\gamma^{2}(1-\gamma)}(I-\gamma P^{\star})\widehat{V}+\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}(1-\gamma)}b^{\star}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle d^{\star},\frac{1}{\gamma}\big(\gamma P^{\star}-I\big)\big(\widehat{V}\circ\widehat{V}\big)+\frac{2}{\gamma^{2}(1-\gamma)}(I-\gamma P^{\star})\widehat{V}+\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}(1-\gamma)}b^{\star}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=d^{\star\top}\big(I-\gamma P^{\star}\big)\left\{-\frac{1}{\gamma}\widehat{V}\circ\widehat{V}+\frac{2}{\gamma^{2}(1-\gamma)}\widehat{V}\right\}+\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}(1-\gamma)}\langle d^{\star},b^{\star}\rangle

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(iv)}}{\leq}(1-\gamma)\rho^{\top}\left\{-\frac{1}{\gamma}\widehat{V}\circ\widehat{V}+\frac{2}{\gamma^{2}(1-\gamma)}\widehat{V}\right\}+\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}(1-\gamma)}\left\langle d^{\star},b^{\star}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\gamma^{2}}\rho^{\top}\widehat{V}+\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}(1-\gamma)}\left\langle d^{\star},b^{\star}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(v)}}{\leq}\frac{2}{\gamma^{2}(1-\gamma)}+\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}(1-\gamma)}\left\langle d^{\star},b^{\star}\right\rangle.

Here, (i) follows by invoking the definition (
8
), (ii) holds due to (
108
), (iii) is valid since
\gamma<1
, (iv) is a direct consequence of (
92
), while (v) comes from the basic facts
\|\rho^{\top}\|_{1}=1
and
\|\widehat{V}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{1-\gamma}
.

7
Analysis: episodic finite-horizon MDPs

7.1
Preliminary facts and notation

We first collect a few preliminary facts that are useful for the analysis. The first fact determines the range of our estimates
\widehat{Q}_{h}
and
\widehat{V}_{h}
.

Lemma 6
.

The iterates of Algorithm
2
obey

\displaystyle 0\leq\widehat{Q}_{h}(s,a)\leq H-h+1\quad\text{and}\quad 0\leq\widehat{V}_{h}(s)\leq H-h+1\qquad\text{for all }(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times[H].

(109)

Proof.

The non-negativity of
\widehat{Q}_{h}
(and hence
\widehat{V}_{h}
) follows directly from the update rule (
52
). Regarding the upper bound, we suppose for the moment that
\widehat{V}_{h+1}(s)\leq H-h
for step
h+1
. Then (
52
) tells us that

\widehat{Q}_{h}(s,a)\leq 1+\big\|\widehat{V}_{h+1}\big\|_{\infty}\leq 1+H-h,

which together with
\widehat{V}_{h}(s)=\max_{a}\widehat{Q}_{h}(s,a)
justifies the claim (
109
) for step
h
as well. Taking this together with the base case
\widehat{V}_{H+1}=0
and the standard induction argument concludes the proof.
∎

The second fact is concerned with the vector
d_{h}^{\star}\coloneqq[d_{h}^{\star}(s)]_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\in\mathbb{R}^{S}
.
For any
h\in[H]
, denote by
P_{h}^{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{S\times S}
a matrix whose
s
-th row is given by
P_{h}\big(\cdot\,|\,s,\pi_{h}^{\star}(s)\big)
.
Then the Markovian property of the MDP indicates that: for any
j>h
, one has

\displaystyle\big(d_{j}^{\star}\big)^{\top}=\big(d_{h}^{\star}\big)^{\top}P_{h}^{\star}\cdots P_{j-1}^{\star}.

(110)

Notation.

We remind the reader that
P_{h,s,a}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times S}
represents the probability transition vector
P_{h}(\cdot\,|\,s,a)
, and the associated variance parameter
\mathsf{Var}_{P_{h,s,a}}(V)
is defined to be the
(h,s,a)
-th row of
\mathsf{Var}_{P}(V)
(cf. (
8
)), namely,

\mathsf{Var}_{P_{h,s,a}}(V)\coloneqq\sum_{s^{\prime}\in S}P_{h}(s^{\prime}\,|\,s,a)\big(V(s^{\prime})\big)^{2}-\bigg(\sum_{s^{\prime}\in S}P_{h}(s^{\prime}\,|\,s,a)V(s^{\prime})\bigg)^{2}

(111)

for any given vector
V\in\mathbb{R}^{S}
.
The vector
\widehat{P}_{h,s,a}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times S}
and the variance parameter
\mathsf{Var}_{\widehat{P}_{h,s,a}}(V)

are defined analogously.

7.2
A crucial statistical independence property

This subsection demonstrates that the subsampling trick introduced in Section
3.3
leads to some crucial statistical independence property.
To be precise, let us consider the following two data-generating mechanisms; here and below, a sample transition refers to a quadruple
(s,a,h,s^{\prime})
that indicates a transition from state
s
to state
s^{\prime}
when action
a
is taken at step
h
.

•

Model 1 (augmented dataset).
Augment
\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{trim}}
to yield a dataset
\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{trim,aug}}
via the following steps. For every
(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times[H]
:

1)

Add to
\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{trim,aug}}
all
N^{\mathsf{trim}}_{h}(s)
sample transitions in
\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{trim}}
that transition from state
s
at step
h
;

2)

If
N^{\mathsf{trim}}_{h}(s)>N^{\mathsf{main}}_{h}(s)
, then we further add to
\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{trim,aug}}

another set of
N^{\mathsf{trim}}_{h}(s)-N^{\mathsf{main}}_{h}(s)
independent sample transitions
\big\{\big(s,a^{(i)}_{h,s},h,s^{\prime\,(i)}_{h,s}\big)\big\}
obeying

\displaystyle a^{(i)}_{h,s}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\pi^{\mathsf{b}}_{h}(\cdot\,|\,s),\qquad s^{\prime\,(i)}_{h,s}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}P_{h}\big(\cdot\,|\,s,a^{(i)}_{h,s}\big),\qquad N^{\mathsf{main}}_{h}(s)<i\leq N^{\mathsf{trim}}_{h}(s).

(112)

•

Model 2 (independent dataset).
For every
(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times[H]
, generate
N^{\mathsf{trim}}_{h}(s)
independent sample transitions
\big\{\big(s,a^{(i)}_{h,s},h,s^{\prime\,(i)}_{h,s}\big)\big\}
as follows:

\displaystyle a^{(i)}_{h,s}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\pi^{\mathsf{b}}_{h}(\cdot\,|\,s),\qquad s^{\prime\,(i)}_{h,s}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}P_{h}(\cdot\,|\,s,a),\qquad 1\leq i\leq N^{\mathsf{trim}}_{h}(s).

(113)

This forms the following dataset:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{i.i.d.}}\coloneqq\Big\{\big(s,a^{(i)}_{h,s},h,s^{\prime\,(i)}_{h,s}\big)\mid s\in\mathcal{S},1\leq h\leq H,1\leq i\leq N^{\mathsf{trim}}_{h}(s)\Big\}.

(114)

In words, the dataset
\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{trim,aug}}
generated in Model 1 differs from
\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{trim}}
only if
N^{\mathsf{trim}}_{h}(s)>N^{\mathsf{main}}_{h}(s)
occurs; this data generating mechanism ensures that
\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{trim,aug}}
comprises exactly
N^{\mathsf{trim}}_{h}(s)
sample transitions from state
s
at step
h
.
Two key features are: (a) the samples in
\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{trim,aug}}
are statistically independent,
and (b)
\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{trim,aug}}
is essentially equivalent to
\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{trim}}
with high probability, as asserted below.

Lemma 7
.

The above two datasets
\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{trim,aug}}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{i.i.d.}}
have the same distributions.
In addition, with probability exceeding
1-8\delta
,
\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{trim,aug}}=\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{trim}}
.

Proof.

Both
\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{trim,aug}}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{i.i.d.}}
contain exactly
N^{\mathsf{trim}}_{h}(s)
sample transitions from state
s
at step
h
.
where
\{N^{\mathsf{trim}}_{h}(s)\}
are statistically independent from the randomness of the samples.
It is easily seen that: given
\{N^{\mathsf{trim}}_{h}(s)\}
, the sample transitions in
\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{trim,aug}}
across different steps are statistically independent. As a result,

\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{trim}}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{i.i.d.}}
both consist of independent samples and are of the same distribution.

Furthermore, Lemma
3
tells us that with probability at least
1-8\delta
,
N^{\mathsf{trim}}_{h}(s)\leq N^{\mathsf{main}}_{h}(s)
holds for all
(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times[H]
, implying that that all data in
\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{trim,aug}}
come from
\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{main}}
and hence
\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{trim,aug}}=\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{trim}}
.
∎

7.3
Proof of Theorem
3

We first demonstrate that Theorem
3
is valid as long as the following theorem can be established.

Theorem 6
.

Consider the dataset
\mathcal{D}_{0}
described in Section
3.2
, and any
0<\delta<1
. Suppose that
\mathcal{D}_{0}
contains
N
sample transitions, and that the non-negative integers
\{N_{h}(s,a)\}
defined in (
50
) obey

\displaystyle\forall(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times[H]:\qquad N_{h}(s,a)\geq\frac{Kd^{\mathsf{b}}_{h}(s,a)}{8}-5\sqrt{Kd^{\mathsf{b}}_{h}(s,a)\log\frac{NH}{\delta}},

(115)

with
K
some quantity obeying
K\geq 3872HSC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NH}{\delta}
.
Assume that conditional on
\{N_{h}(s,a)\}
, the sample transitions
\{(s,a,h,s^{\prime}_{(i)})\,|\,1\leq i\leq N_{h}(s,a),(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times[H]\}
are statistically independent.
The penalty terms are taken to be (
55
), where

c_{\mathsf{b}}\geq 16
is chosen to be some constant.
Then with probability at least
1-4\delta
, one has

\displaystyle\sum_{s}d_{h}^{\star}(s)\big(V^{\star}_{h}(s)-V^{\widehat{\pi}}_{h}(s)\big)\leq 80\sqrt{\frac{2c_{\mathsf{b}}H^{3}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NH}{\delta}}{K}},\qquad 1\leq h\leq H.

(116)

By construction,
\{N^{\mathsf{trim}}_{h}(s,a)\}
are computed using
\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{aux}}
, and hence are independent from the empirical model
\widehat{P}_{h}
generated based on
\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{trim}}
.
Additionally, Lemma
7
permits us to treat the samples in
\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{trim}}
as being statistically independent.
Recalling that the lower bound (
57b
) holds with probability at least
1-8\delta
, we can readily invoke Theorem
6
by taking
N_{h}(s,a)=N^{\mathsf{trim}}_{h}(s,a)
and the property (
42
) to show that

\displaystyle\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\rho(s)\big(V^{\star}_{1}(s)-V^{\widehat{\pi}}_{1}(s)\big)=\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}d_{1}^{\star}(s)\big(V^{\star}_{1}(s)-V^{\widehat{\pi}}_{1}(s)\big)\leq 80\sqrt{\frac{2c_{\mathsf{b}}H^{3}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NH}{\delta}}{K}}

(117)

with probability at least
1-12\delta
, provided that
K\geq 3872HSC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{KH}{\delta}
.
Setting the right-hand side of (
117
) to be smaller than
\varepsilon
immediately concludes the proof of Theorem
3
, where we have used the fact that
N\leq KH
in
\mathcal{D}_{0}
.
As a consequence, it suffices to establish Theorem
6
.
In the sequel, we shall assume without loss of generality that we are working on the high-probability event (
57
).

7.3.1
Proof of Theorem
6

Step 1: showing that
\widehat{Q}_{h}(s,a)\leq Q_{h}^{\widehat{\pi}}(s,a)
.

This part relies crucially on the following lemma.

Lemma 8
.

Consider any
1\leq h\leq H
, and any vector
V\in\mathbb{R}^{S}
independent of
\widehat{P}_{h}
obeying
\|V\|_{\infty}\leq H
.
With probability at least
1-4\delta/H
, one has

\displaystyle\big|\big(\widehat{P}_{h,s,a}-P_{h,s,a}\big)V\big|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{48\mathsf{Var}_{\widehat{P}_{h,s,a}}(V)\log\frac{NH}{\delta}}{N_{h}(s,a)}}+\frac{48H\log\frac{NH}{\delta}}{N_{h}(s,a)}

(118)

\displaystyle\mathsf{Var}_{\widehat{P}_{h,s,a}}(V)

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathsf{Var}_{P_{h,s,a}}\big(V\big)+\frac{5H^{2}\log\frac{NH}{\delta}}{3N_{h}(s,a)}

(119)

simultaneously for all
(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
.

Proof.

The proof follows from exactly the same argument as that of Lemma
9
, except that the assumed upper bound on
\|V\|_{\infty}
is now
H
(as opposed to
\frac{1}{1-\gamma}
) and
\delta
is replaced with
\delta/H
. We thus omit the proof details for brevity.
∎

Additionally, we make note of the crude bound
\big|(\widehat{P}_{h,s,a}-P_{h,s,a})\widehat{V}_{h+1}\big|\leq\|\widehat{V}_{h+1}\|_{\infty}\leq H
.
Also, given that Algorithm
2
works backwards,
the iterate
\widehat{V}_{h+1}
does not use
\widehat{P}_{h}
, and is hence statistically independent from
\widehat{P}_{h}
.
Thus, we can readily apply Lemma
8
to obtain

\forall(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times[H]:\quad\big|\big(\widehat{P}_{h,s,a}-P_{h,s,a}\big)\widehat{V}_{h+1}\big|\leq b_{h}(s,a)

(120)

in the presence of the Bernstein-style penalty (
55
),
provided that the constant
c_{\mathsf{b}}>0
is sufficiently large.

In the sequel,
we shall work with the high-probability events (
120
) and
(
119
), in addition to (
57
). We intend to prove the following relation

\displaystyle\forall(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times[H]:\quad\widehat{Q}_{h}(s,a)\leq Q_{h}^{\widehat{\pi}}(s,a)\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{V}_{h}(s)\leq V_{h}^{\widehat{\pi}}(s)

(121)

hold with probability exceeding
1-4\delta
. Note that the latter assertion concerning
\widehat{V}_{h}
is implied by the former, according to the following relation:

\widehat{V}_{h}(s)=\max_{a}\widehat{Q}_{h}(s,a)=\widehat{Q}_{h}\big(s,\widehat{\pi}_{h}(s)\big)\leq Q_{h}^{\widehat{\pi}}\big(s,\widehat{\pi}_{h}(s)\big)=V_{h}^{\widehat{\pi}}(s).

(122)

Therefore, we focus on the first assertion and will show it by induction.
First of all, the claim (
121
) holds trivially for the base case with
h=H+1
, given that
\widehat{Q}_{H+1}(s,a)=Q_{H+1}^{\widehat{\pi}}(s,a)=0
.
Next, suppose that
\widehat{Q}_{h+1}(s,a)\leq Q_{h+1}^{\widehat{\pi}}(s,a)
holds for all
(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
and some step
h+1
.
We would like to show that the claimed inequality holds for step
h
as well.
If
\widehat{Q}_{h}(s,a)=0
, then the claim holds trivially; otherwise, our update rule (
52
) reveals that

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}_{h}(s,a)

\displaystyle=r_{h}(s,a)+\widehat{P}_{h,s,a}\widehat{V}_{h+1}-b_{h}(s,a)

\displaystyle=r_{h}(s,a)+P_{h,s,a}\widehat{V}_{h+1}+\big(\widehat{P}_{h,s,a}-P_{h,s,a}\big)\widehat{V}_{h+1}-b_{h}(s,a)

\displaystyle\overset{(\mathrm{i})}{\leq}r_{h}(s,a)+P_{h,s,a}V_{h+1}^{\widehat{\pi}}\overset{(\mathrm{ii})}{=}Q_{h}^{\widehat{\pi}}(s,a),

with probability at least
1-\delta/2
,
where (i) results from (
120
) and (
122
) (i.e.,
\widehat{V}_{h+1}(s)\leq V_{h+1}^{\widehat{\pi}}(s)
), and (ii) arises from the Bellman equation.
We have thus established (
121
) via a standard induction argument.

Step 2: bounding
V_{h}^{\star}(s)-V^{\widehat{\pi}}_{h}(s)
.

In view of (
122
), we make the observation that

\displaystyle 0\leq V_{h}^{\star}(s)-V_{h}^{\widehat{\pi}}(s)

\displaystyle\leq V_{h}^{\star}(s)-\widehat{V}_{h}(s)\leq Q_{h}^{\star}\big(s,\pi^{\star}_{h}(s)\big)-\widehat{Q}_{h}\big(s,\pi^{\star}_{h}(s)\big),

(123)

where the last inequality holds true since
V_{h}^{\star}(s)=Q_{h}^{\star}(s,\pi^{\star}_{h}(s))
and
\widehat{V}_{h}(s)=\max_{a}\widehat{Q}_{h}(s,a)\geq\widehat{Q}_{h}(s,\pi^{\star}_{h}(s))
.
Recognizing that

\displaystyle Q_{h}^{\star}\big(s,\pi_{h}^{\star}(s)\big)

\displaystyle=r\big(s,\pi_{h}^{\star}(s)\big)+P_{h,s,\pi_{h}^{\star}(s)}V_{h+1}^{\star},

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}_{h}\big(s,\pi_{h}^{\star}(s)\big)

\displaystyle=\max\Big\{r\big(s,\pi_{h}^{\star}(s)\big)+\widehat{P}_{h,s,\pi_{h}^{\star}(s)}\widehat{V}_{h+1}-b_{h}\big(s,\pi_{h}^{\star}(s)\big),\,0\Big\},

we can continue the derivation of (
123
) to obtain

\displaystyle V_{h}^{\star}(s)-\widehat{V}_{h}(s)

\displaystyle\leq r\big(s,\pi_{h}^{\star}(s)\big)+P_{h,s,\pi_{h}^{\star}(s)}V_{h+1}^{\star}-\left\{r\big(s,\pi_{h}^{\star}(s)\big)+\widehat{P}_{h,s,\pi_{h}^{\star}(s)}\widehat{V}_{h+1}-b_{h}\big(s,\pi_{h}^{\star}(s)\big)\right\}

\displaystyle=P_{h,s,\pi_{h}^{\star}(s)}V_{h+1}^{\star}-\widehat{P}_{h,s,\pi_{h}^{\star}(s)}\widehat{V}_{h+1}+b_{h}\big(s,\pi_{h}^{\star}(s)\big)

\displaystyle=P_{h,s,\pi_{h}^{\star}(s)}\big(V_{h+1}^{\star}-\widehat{V}_{h+1}\big)-\Big(\widehat{P}_{h,s,\pi_{h}^{\star}(s)}-P_{h,s,\pi_{h}^{\star}(s)}\Big)\widehat{V}_{h+1}+b_{h}\big(s,\pi_{h}^{\star}(s)\big)

\displaystyle\leq P_{h,s,\pi_{h}^{\star}(s)}\big(V_{h+1}^{\star}-\widehat{V}_{h+1}\big)+2b_{h}\big(s,\pi_{h}^{\star}(s)\big)

(124)

with probability at least
1-\delta
, where the last inequality is valid due to (
120
).
For notational convenience, let us introduce a sequence of matrices
P_{h}^{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{S\times S}
(
1\leq h\leq H
) and vectors
b_{h}^{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{S}
(
1\leq h\leq H
), with their
s
-th rows given by

\displaystyle\big[P_{h}^{\star}\big]_{s,\cdot}\coloneqq P_{h,s,\pi^{\star}_{h}(s)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad b_{h}^{\star}(s)\coloneqq b_{h}\big(s,\pi^{\star}_{h}(s)\big).

(125)

This allows us to rewrite (
124
) in matrix/vector form as follows:

\displaystyle 0\leq V_{h}^{\star}-\widehat{V}_{h}

\displaystyle\leq P_{h}^{\star}\big(V_{h+1}^{\star}-\widehat{V}_{h+1}\big)+2b_{h}^{\star}.

(126)

The inequality (
126
) plays a key role in the analysis since it establishes a connection between the value estimation errors in step
h
and step
h+1
.

Given that
b_{h}^{\star}
,
P_{h}^{\star}
and
V_{h}^{\star}-\widehat{V}_{h}
are all non-negative,
applying (
126
) recursively with the boundary condition
V_{H+1}^{\star}=\widehat{V}_{H+1}=0
leads to

\displaystyle 0\leq V_{h}^{\star}-\widehat{V}_{h}

\displaystyle\leq P_{h}^{\star}\big(V_{h+1}^{\star}-\widehat{V}_{h+1}\big)+2b_{h}^{\star}

\displaystyle\leq P_{h}^{\star}P_{h+1}^{\star}\big(V_{h+2}^{\star}-\widehat{V}_{h+2}\big)+2P_{h}^{\star}b_{h+1}^{\star}+2b_{h}^{\star}\leq\cdots

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{j=h}^{H}\bigg(\prod_{k=h}^{j-1}P_{k}^{\star}\bigg)b_{j}^{\star},

where we adopt the following notation for convenience (note the order of the product)

\prod_{k=h}^{h-1}P_{k}^{\star}=I\qquad\text{and}\qquad\prod_{k=h}^{j-1}P_{k}^{\star}=P_{h}^{\star}\cdots P_{j-1}^{\star}~~~~\text{if }j>h.

With this inequality in mind, we can let
d_{h}^{\star}\coloneqq[d_{h}^{\star}(s)]_{s\in\mathcal{S}}
be a
S
-dimensional vector and derive

\displaystyle\big\langle d_{h}^{\star},V_{h}^{\star}-V_{h}^{\widehat{\pi}}\big\rangle\leq\big\langle d_{h}^{\star},V_{h}^{\star}-\widehat{V}_{h}\big\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\Bigg<d_{h}^{\star},2\sum_{j=h}^{H}\bigg(\prod_{k=h}^{j-1}P_{k}^{\star}\bigg)b_{j}^{\star}\Bigg>

\displaystyle=2\sum_{j=h}^{H}\big(d_{h}^{\star}\big)^{\top}\bigg(\prod_{k=h}^{j-1}P_{k}^{\star}\bigg)b_{j}^{\star}=2\sum_{j=h}^{H}\big\langle d_{j}^{\star},b_{j}^{\star}\big\rangle,

(127)

where we have made use of (
123
) and the elementary identity (
110
).

Step 3: using concentrability to bound
\langle d_{j}^{\star},b_{j}^{\star}\rangle
.

To finish up, we need to make use of the concentrability coefficient. In what follows, we look at two cases separately.

•

Case 1:
Kd^{\mathsf{b}}_{j}\big(s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)\big)\leq 4c_{\mathsf{b}}\log\frac{NH}{\delta}
.
Given that
b_{h}(s,a)\leq H
(cf. (
55
)), we necessarily have

\displaystyle b_{j}^{\star}(s)\leq H\leq H\cdot\frac{4c_{\mathsf{b}}\log\frac{NH}{\delta}}{Kd^{\mathsf{b}}_{j}\big(s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)\big)}\leq\frac{4c_{\mathsf{b}}C^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}H\log\frac{NH}{\delta}}{K\min\big\{d_{j}^{\star}(s),\frac{1}{S}\big\}}

(128)

in this case, where the last inequality arises from Definition
4
.

•

Case 2:
Kd^{\mathsf{b}}_{j}\big(s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)\big)>4c_{\mathsf{b}}\log\frac{NH}{\delta}
.
It follows from the assumption (
115
) that

\displaystyle N_{j}\big(s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)\big)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{Kd^{\mathsf{b}}_{j}\big(s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)\big)}{8}-5\sqrt{Kd^{\mathsf{b}}_{j}\big(s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)\big)\log\frac{N}{\delta}}\geq\frac{Kd^{\mathsf{b}}_{j}\big(s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)\big)}{16}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{K\min\big\{d_{j}^{\star}\big(s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)\big),\frac{1}{S}\big\}}{16C^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}}=\frac{K\min\big\{d_{j}^{\star}(s),\frac{1}{S}\big\}}{16C^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}},

(129)

as long as
c_{\mathsf{b}}>0
is sufficiently large. Here, the last line results from Definition
4

and the assumption that
\pi^{\star}
is a deterministic policy (so that
d_{j}^{\star}(s)=d_{j}^{\star}\big(s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)\big)
).

This further leads to

\displaystyle b_{j}^{\star}(s)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{c_{\mathsf{b}}\log\frac{NH}{\delta}}{N_{j}\big(s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)\big)}\mathsf{Var}_{\widehat{P}_{j,s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)}}(\widehat{V}_{j+1})}+c_{\mathsf{b}}H\frac{\log\frac{NH}{\delta}}{N_{j}\big(s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)\big)}

\displaystyle\overset{(\mathrm{i})}{\leq}\sqrt{\frac{2c_{\mathsf{b}}\log\frac{NH}{\delta}}{N_{j}\big(s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)\big)}\mathsf{Var}_{P_{j,s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)}}(\widehat{V}_{j+1})}+3c_{\mathsf{b}}H\frac{\log\frac{NH}{\delta}}{N_{j}\big(s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)\big)}

\displaystyle\overset{(\mathrm{ii})}{\leq}\sqrt{\frac{32c_{\mathsf{b}}C^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NH}{\delta}}{K\min\big\{d_{j}^{\star}(s),\frac{1}{S}\big\}}\mathsf{Var}_{P_{j,s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)}}(\widehat{V}_{j+1})}+48c_{\mathsf{b}}C^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}H\frac{\log\frac{NH}{\delta}}{K\min\big\{d_{j}^{\star}(s),\frac{1}{S}\big\}}.

Here, (i) comes from (
119
) and the elementary inequality
\sqrt{x+y}\leq\sqrt{x}+\sqrt{y}
for any
x,y\geq 0
, provided that
c_{\mathsf{b}}
is large enough; and (ii) relies on (
129
).

Putting the above two cases together, we arrive at

\displaystyle\sum_{s}d_{j}^{\star}(s)b_{j}^{\star}(s)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s}d_{j}^{\star}(s)\sqrt{\frac{32c_{\mathsf{b}}C^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NH}{\delta}}{K\min\big\{d_{j}^{\star}(s),\frac{1}{S}\big\}}\mathsf{Var}_{P_{j,s,\pi^{\star}_{j}(s)}}(\widehat{V}_{j+1})}+48c_{\mathsf{b}}H\sum_{s}d_{j}^{\star}(s)\frac{C^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NH}{\delta}}{K\min\big\{d_{j}^{\star}(s),\frac{1}{S}\big\}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s}d_{j}^{\star}(s)\sqrt{\frac{32c_{\mathsf{b}}C^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NH}{\delta}}{K\min\big\{d_{j}^{\star}(s),\frac{1}{S}\big\}}\mathsf{Var}_{P_{j,s,\pi^{\star}_{j}(s)}}(\widehat{V}_{j+1})}+\frac{96c_{\mathsf{b}}HSC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NH}{\delta}}{K},

(130)

where the last inequality holds since

\displaystyle\sum_{s}\frac{d_{j}^{\star}(s)}{\min\big\{d_{j}^{\star}(s),\frac{1}{S}\big\}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s}d_{j}^{\star}(s)\bigg\{\frac{1}{d_{j}^{\star}(s)}+\frac{1}{1/S}\bigg\}\leq\sum_{s}1+S\sum_{s}d_{j}^{\star}(s)\leq 2S.

In addition, we make the observation that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=h}^{H}\sum_{s}d_{j}^{\star}(s)\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{Var}_{P_{j,s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)}}\big(\widehat{V}_{j+1}\big)}{\min\big\{d_{j}^{\star}(s),\frac{1}{S}\big\}}}\leq\sum_{j=h}^{H}\sum_{s}d_{j}^{\star}(s)\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{Var}_{P_{j,s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)}}\big(\widehat{V}_{j+1}\big)}{d_{j}^{\star}(s)}}+\sum_{j=h}^{H}\sum_{s}d_{j}^{\star}(s)\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{Var}_{P_{j,s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)}}\big(\widehat{V}_{j+1}\big)}{1/S}}

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{j=h}^{H}\sum_{s}\sqrt{d_{j}^{\star}(s)\mathsf{Var}_{P_{j,s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)}}\big(\widehat{V}_{j+1}\big)}+\sqrt{S}\sum_{j=h}^{H}\sum_{s}d_{j}^{\star}(s)\sqrt{\mathsf{Var}_{P_{j,s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)}}\big(\widehat{V}_{j+1}\big)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\sqrt{HS}\cdot\sqrt{\sum_{j=h}^{H}\sum_{s}d_{j}^{\star}(s)\mathsf{Var}_{P_{j,s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)}}\big(\widehat{V}_{j+1}\big)}+\sqrt{S}\sqrt{\sum_{j=h}^{H}\sum_{s}d_{j}^{\star}(s)}\sqrt{\sum_{j=h}^{H}\sum_{s}d_{j}^{\star}(s)\mathsf{Var}_{P_{j,s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)}}\big(\widehat{V}_{j+1}\big)}

\displaystyle\quad=2\sqrt{HS}\cdot\sqrt{\sum_{j=h}^{H}\sum_{s}d_{j}^{\star}(s)\mathsf{Var}_{P_{j,s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)}}\big(\widehat{V}_{j+1}\big)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq 4\sqrt{HS\bigg(H^{2}+H\sum_{j=h}^{H}\big\langle d_{j}^{\star},b_{j}^{\star}\big\rangle\bigg)}\leq 4\sqrt{H^{3}S}+4\sqrt{H^{2}S\sum_{j=h}^{H}\big\langle d_{j}^{\star},b_{j}^{\star}\big\rangle},

where the third line makes use of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,
and the last line would hold as long as we could establish the following inequality

\displaystyle\sum_{j=h}^{H}\sum_{s}d_{j}^{\star}(s)\mathsf{Var}_{P_{j,s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)}}\big(\widehat{V}_{j+1}\big)\leq 4H^{2}+4H\sum_{j=h}^{H}\big\langle d_{j}^{\star},b_{j}^{\star}\big\rangle

(131)

for all
h\in[H]
with probability exceeding
1-4\delta
.
Substitution into (
130
) yields

\displaystyle\sum_{j=h}^{H}\sum_{s}d_{j}^{\star}(s)b_{j}^{\star}(s)\leq\sqrt{\frac{32c_{\mathsf{b}}C^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NH}{\delta}}{K}}\left\{4\sqrt{H^{3}S}+4\sqrt{H^{2}S\sum_{j=h}^{H}\big\langle d_{j}^{\star},b_{j}^{\star}\big\rangle}\right\}+\sum_{j=h}^{H}\frac{96c_{\mathsf{b}}HSC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NH}{\delta}}{K}

\displaystyle\quad\leq 16\sqrt{\frac{2c_{\mathsf{b}}H^{2}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NH}{\delta}}{K}}\sqrt{\sum_{j=h}^{H}\big\langle d_{j}^{\star},b_{j}^{\star}\big\rangle}+16\sqrt{\frac{2c_{\mathsf{b}}H^{3}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NH}{\delta}}{K}}+\frac{96c_{\mathsf{b}}H^{2}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NH}{\delta}}{K}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=h}^{H}\big\langle d_{j}^{\star},b_{j}^{\star}\big\rangle+\frac{256c_{\mathsf{b}}H^{2}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NH}{\delta}}{K}+16\sqrt{\frac{2c_{\mathsf{b}}H^{3}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NH}{\delta}}{K}}+\frac{96c_{\mathsf{b}}H^{2}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NH}{\delta}}{K},

where the last inequality follows from the elementary inequality
2xy\leq x^{2}+y^{2}
.
Rearranging terms, we are left with

\displaystyle\sum_{j=h}^{H}\sum_{s}d_{j}^{\star}(s)b_{j}^{\star}(s)

\displaystyle\leq 32\sqrt{\frac{2c_{\mathsf{b}}H^{3}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NH}{\delta}}{K}}+\frac{704c_{\mathsf{b}}H^{2}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NH}{\delta}}{K}

\displaystyle\leq 40\sqrt{\frac{2c_{\mathsf{b}}H^{3}SC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NH}{\delta}}{K}},

provided that
K\geq 3872HSC^{\star}_{\mathsf{clipped}}\log\frac{NH}{\delta}
.
This taken collectively with (
127
) completes the proof of Theorem
6
,
as long as the inequality (
131
) can be validated.

Proof of inequality (
131
).

First of all, we observe that

\displaystyle\widehat{V}_{j}(s)+2b_{j}^{\star}\big(s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)\big)-P_{j,s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)}\widehat{V}_{j+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{V}_{j}(s)-\widehat{P}_{j,s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)}\widehat{V}_{j+1}+2b_{j}^{\star}\big(s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)\big)+\big(\widehat{P}_{j,s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)}-P_{j,s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)}\big)\widehat{V}_{j+1}

\displaystyle\overset{(\mathrm{i})}{\geq}\widehat{V}_{j}(s)-\widehat{P}_{j,s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)}\widehat{V}_{j+1}+b_{j}^{\star}\big(s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)\big)

\displaystyle\geq\widehat{V}_{j}(s)-\Big\{r\big(s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)\big)+\widehat{P}_{j,s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)}\widehat{V}_{j+1}-b_{j}^{\star}\big(s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)\big)\Big\}

\displaystyle\geq\max_{a}\widehat{Q}_{j}(s,a)-\widehat{Q}_{j}\big(s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)\big)\geq 0

for any
s\in\mathcal{S}
, where (i) is a consequence of (
120
), and the last line arises from (
52
) and (
53
).
This implies the non-negativity
of the vector
\widehat{V}_{j}+2b_{j}^{\star}-P_{j}^{\star}\widehat{V}_{j+1}
,
which in turn allows one to deduce that

\displaystyle\widehat{V}_{j}\circ\widehat{V}_{j}-\big(P_{j}^{\star}\widehat{V}_{j+1}\big)\circ\big(P_{j}^{\star}\widehat{V}_{j+1}\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\widehat{V}_{j}+P_{j}^{\star}\widehat{V}_{j+1}\big)\circ\big(\widehat{V}_{j}-P_{j}^{\star}\widehat{V}_{j+1}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\big(\widehat{V}_{j}+P_{j}^{\star}\widehat{V}_{j+1}\big)\circ\big(\widehat{V}_{j}+2b_{j}^{\star}-P_{j}^{\star}\widehat{V}_{j+1}\big)

\displaystyle\leq 2H\big(\widehat{V}_{j}+2b_{j}^{\star}-P_{j}^{\star}\widehat{V}_{j+1}\big),

(132)

where the last line relies on Lemma
6
.
Consequently, we can demonstrate that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=h}^{H}\sum_{s}d_{j}^{\star}(s)\mathsf{Var}_{P_{j,s,\pi_{j}^{\star}(s)}}\big(\widehat{V}_{j+1}\big)=\sum_{j=h}^{H}\big\langle d_{j}^{\star},P_{j}^{\star}\big(\widehat{V}_{j+1}\circ\widehat{V}_{j+1}\big)-\big(P_{j}^{\star}\widehat{V}_{j+1}\big)\circ\big(P_{j}^{\star}\widehat{V}_{j+1}\big)\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=h}^{H}\big(d_{j}^{\star}\big)^{\top}P_{j}^{\star}\widehat{V}_{j+1}\circ\widehat{V}_{j+1}-\big\langle d_{j}^{\star},\big(P_{j}^{\star}\widehat{V}_{j+1}\big)\circ\big(P_{j}^{\star}\widehat{V}_{j+1}\big)\big\rangle

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(i)}}{\leq}\sum_{j=h}^{H}\Big(\big\langle d_{j+1}^{\star},\widehat{V}_{j+1}\circ\widehat{V}_{j+1}\big\rangle-\big\langle d_{j}^{\star},\widehat{V}_{j}\circ\widehat{V}_{j}\big\rangle+2H\big\langle d_{j}^{\star},\widehat{V}_{j}+2b_{j}^{\star}-P_{j}^{\star}\widehat{V}_{j+1}\big\rangle\Big)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(ii)}}{=}\sum_{j=h}^{H}\Big(\big\langle d_{j+1}^{\star},\widehat{V}_{j+1}\circ\widehat{V}_{j+1}\big\rangle-\big\langle d_{j}^{\star},\widehat{V}_{j}\circ\widehat{V}_{j}\big\rangle\Big)+2H\sum_{j=h}^{H}\Big(\big\langle d_{j}^{\star},\widehat{V}_{j}\big\rangle-\big\langle d_{j+1}^{\star},\widehat{V}_{j+1}\big\rangle\Big)+4H\sum_{j=h}^{H}\big\langle d_{j}^{\star},b_{j}^{\star}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\big\langle d_{H+1}^{\star},\widehat{V}_{H+1}\circ\widehat{V}_{H+1}\big\rangle-\big\langle d_{h}^{\star},\widehat{V}_{h}\circ\widehat{V}_{h}\big\rangle+2H\Big(\big\langle d_{h}^{\star},\widehat{V}_{h}\big\rangle-\big\langle d_{H+1}^{\star},\widehat{V}_{H+1}\big\rangle\Big)+4H\sum_{j=h}^{H}\big\langle d_{j}^{\star},b_{j}^{\star}\big\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\big\|d_{H+1}^{\star}\big\|_{1}\big\|\widehat{V}_{H+1}\circ\widehat{V}_{H+1}\big\|_{\infty}+2H\big\|d_{h}^{\star}\big\|_{1}\big\|\widehat{V}_{h}\big\|_{\infty}+4H\sum_{j=h}^{H}\big\langle d_{j}^{\star},b_{j}^{\star}\big\rangle

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(iii)}}{\leq}3H^{2}+4H\sum_{j=h}^{H}\big\langle d_{j}^{\star},b_{j}^{\star}\big\rangle,

where (i) arises from (
132
) as well as the basic property
\big(d_{j}^{\star}\big)^{\top}P_{j}^{\star}=\big(d_{j+1}^{\star}\big)^{\top}
,
(ii) follows by rearranging terms and using the property
\big(d_{j}^{\star}\big)^{\top}P_{j}^{\star}=\big(d_{j+1}^{\star}\big)^{\top}
once again,
and (iii) holds due to the fact that
\|\widehat{V}_{h}\|_{\infty}\leq H
and
\|d_{h}^{\star}\|_{1}=1
.
This concludes the proof of (
131
).

8
Discussion

Our primary contribution has been to pin down the sample complexity of model-based offline RL for the tabular settings,
by establishing its (near) minimax optimality for both infinite- and finite-horizon MDPs.
While reliable estimation of the transition kernel is often infeasible in the sample-starved regime,
it does not preclude the success of this “plug-in” approach in learning the optimal policy.
Encouragingly, the sample complexity characterization we have derived holds for the entire range of target accuracy level
\varepsilon
,
thus revealing that sample optimality comes into effect without incurring any burn-in cost.
This is in stark contrast to all prior results, which either suffered from sample sub-optimality or required a large burn-in sample size in order to yield optimal efficiency.
We have demonstrated that sophisticated techniques like variance reduction are not necessary, as long as Bernstein-style lower confidence bounds are carefully employed to capture the variance of the estimates in each iteration.

Turning to future directions, we first note that the two-fold subsampling adopted in Algorithm
3
is likely unnecessary; it would be of interest to develop sharp analysis for the VI-LCB algorithm without sample splitting, which would call for more refined analysis in order to handle the complicated statistical dependency between different time steps.
Notably, while avoiding sample splitting cannot improve the sample complexity in an order-wise sense,
the potential gain in terms of the pre-constants as well as the algoritmic simplicity might be of practical interest.
Moreover, given the appealing memory efficiency of model-free algorithms,
understanding whether one can design sample-optimal model-free offline algorithms with minimal burn-in periods
is another open direction. Moving beyond tabular settings, it would be of great interest to extend our analysis to
accommodate model-based offline RL in more general scenarios; examples include MDPs with low-complexity linear representations, and offline RL involving multiple agents.

Appendix A
Proof of auxiliary lemmas: infinite-horizon MDPs

A.1
Proof of Lemma
1

Before embarking on the proof, we introduce several notation.
To make explicit the dependency on
V
, we shall express the penalty term using the following notation throughout this subsection:

\displaystyle b(s,a;V)=\min\Bigg\{\max\bigg\{\sqrt{\frac{c_{\mathsf{b}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{N(s,a)}\mathsf{Var}_{\widehat{P}_{s,a}}(V)},\,\frac{2c_{\mathsf{b}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)N(s,a)}\bigg\},\,\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\Bigg\}+\frac{5}{N}

(133)

For any
Q,Q_{1},Q_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{SA}
, we write

\displaystyle V(s)\coloneqq\max_{a}Q(s,a),\qquad V_{1}(s)\coloneqq\max_{a}Q_{1}(s,a)\qquad\text{and}\qquad V_{2}(s)\coloneqq\max_{a}Q_{2}(s,a)

(134)

for all
s\in\mathcal{S}
.
Unless otherwise noted, we assume that

Q(s,a),Q_{1}(s,a),Q_{2}(s,a)\in\Big[0,\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\Big]\qquad\text{for all }(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}

throughout this subsection.
In addition, let us define another operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pe}}
obeying

\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pe}}(Q)(s,a)=r(s,a)-b(s,a;V)+\gamma\widehat{P}_{s,a}V\qquad\text{for all }(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}

(135)

for any
Q\in\mathbb{R}^{SA}
.
It is self-evident that

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pe}}(Q)(s,a)=\max\big\{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pe}}(Q)(s,a),0\big\}\qquad\text{for all }(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}.

(136)

\gamma
-contraction.

The main step of the proof lies in showing the monotonicity of the operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pe}}
in the sense that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pe}}(Q)\leq\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pe}}(\widetilde{Q})\qquad\text{for any }Q\leq\widetilde{Q}.

(137)

Suppose that this claim is valid for the moment, then one can demonstrate that: for any
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{SA}
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pe}}(Q_{1})-\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pe}}(Q_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pe}}\big(Q_{2}+\|Q_{1}-Q_{2}\|_{\infty}1\big)-\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pe}}(Q_{2}),

(138a)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pe}}(Q_{1})-\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pe}}(Q_{2})

\displaystyle\geq\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pe}}\big(Q_{2}-\|Q_{1}-Q_{2}\|_{\infty}1\big)-\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pe}}(Q_{2}),

(138b)

with
1
denoting the all-one vector. Additionally, observe that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Var}_{\widehat{P}_{s,a}}(V)=\mathsf{Var}_{\widehat{P}_{s,a}}(V+c\cdot 1)\qquad\text{and hence}\qquad b(s,a;V)=b(s,a;V+c\cdot 1)

for any constant
c
, which together with the identity
\widehat{P}1=1
immediately leads to

\displaystyle\Big\|\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pe}}\big(Q_{2}-\|Q_{1}-Q_{2}\|_{\infty}1\big)-\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pe}}(Q_{2})\Big\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\Big\|\widehat{P}\big(\|Q_{1}-Q_{2}\|_{\infty}1\big)\Big\|_{\infty}=\gamma\|Q_{1}-Q_{2}\|_{\infty},

\displaystyle\Big\|\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pe}}\big(Q_{2}+\|Q_{1}-Q_{2}\|_{\infty}1\big)-\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pe}}(Q_{2})\Big\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\Big\|\widehat{P}\big(\|Q_{1}-Q_{2}\|_{\infty}1\big)\Big\|_{\infty}=\gamma\|Q_{1}-Q_{2}\|_{\infty}.

Taking this together with (
138
) yields

\displaystyle\big\|\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pe}}(Q_{1})-\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pe}}(Q_{2})\big\|_{\infty}\leq\gamma\|Q_{1}-Q_{2}\|_{\infty},

which combined with the basic property

\big\|\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pe}}(Q_{1})-\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pe}}(Q_{2})\big\|_{\infty}\leq\big\|\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pe}}(Q_{1})-\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pe}}(Q_{2})\big\|_{\infty}
(as a result of (
136
)) justifies that

\displaystyle\big\|\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pe}}(Q_{1})-\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pe}}(Q_{2})\big\|_{\infty}\leq\gamma\|Q_{1}-Q_{2}\|_{\infty}.

(139)

The remainder of the proof is thus devoted to establishing the monotonicity property (
137
).

Proof of the monotonicity property (
137
).

Consider any point
Q\in\mathbb{R}^{SA}
, and we would like to examine the derivative of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pe}}
at point
Q
.
Towards this end, we consider any
(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
and divide into several cases.

•

Case 1:
\max\bigg\{\sqrt{\frac{c_{\mathsf{b}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{N(s,a)}\mathsf{Var}_{\widehat{P}_{s,a}}(V)},\,\frac{2c_{\mathsf{b}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)N(s,a)}\bigg\}>\frac{1}{1-\gamma}
.
In this case, the penalty term (
133
) simplifies to

b(s,a;V)=\frac{1}{1-\gamma}+\frac{5}{N}.

Taking the derivative of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pe}}(Q)(s,a)
w.r.t. the
s^{\prime}
-th component of
V
leads to

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\big(\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{pe}}(Q)(s,a)\big)}{\partial V(s^{\prime})}=\frac{\partial\big(r(s,a)-\frac{1}{1-\gamma}+\gamma\widehat{P}_{s,a}V\big)}{\partial V(s^{\prime})}=\gamma\widehat{P}(s^{\prime}\,|\,s,a)\geq 0

(140)

for any
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}
.

•

Case 2:
\sqrt{\frac{c_{\mathsf{b}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{N(s,a)}\mathsf{Var}_{\widehat{P}_{s,a}}(V)}<\frac{2c_{\mathsf{b}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)N(s,a)}<\frac{1}{1-\gamma}
.

The penalty (
133
) in this case reduces to

b(s,a;V)=\frac{2c_{\mathsf{b}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)N(s,a)}+\frac{5}{N},

an expression that is independent of
V
. As a result, repeating the argument for Case 1 indicates that (
140
) continues to hold for this case.

•

Case 3:

\frac{2c_{\mathsf{b}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)N(s,a)}<\sqrt{\frac{c_{\mathsf{b}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{N(s,a)}\mathsf{Var}_{\widehat{P}_{s,a}}(V)}<\frac{1}{1-\gamma}
.
In this case, the penalty term is given by

b(s,a;V)=\sqrt{\frac{c_{\mathsf{b}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{N(s,a)}\mathsf{Var}_{\widehat{P}_{s,a}}(V)}+\frac{5}{N}.

Note that in this case, we necessarily have

\displaystyle\mathsf{Var}_{\widehat{P}_{s,a}}(V)\geq\frac{4c_{\mathsf{b}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)^{2}N(s,a)},

which together with the definition in (
8
) indicates that

\displaystyle\widehat{P}_{s,a}(V\circ V)-\big(\widehat{P}_{s,a}V\big)^{2}\geq\frac{4c_{\mathsf{b}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{(1-\gamma)^{2}N(s,a)}>0.

(141)

As a result, for any
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}
, taking the derivative of
b(s,a;V)
w.r.t. the
s^{\prime}
-th component of
V
gives

\displaystyle\frac{\partial b(s,a;V)}{\partial V(s^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{c_{\mathsf{b}}\log\frac{N}{(1-\gamma)\delta}}{N(s,a)}}\frac{\partial\sqrt{\widehat{P}_{s,a}(V\circ V)-\big(\widehat{P}_{s,a}V\big)^{2}}}{\partial V(s^{\prime})}
